Regimental Marches Of British Army

LINK TO MELODIC CHAIN 


The following article has been suggested by a remark of an 
elderly jady at the Cenotaph on the occasion ef the King's Birthday 


' Parade on Monday last when she asked a companion: “Why do not 


all the sailors and soldiers march to the tune of the National An- 


; them, “God Save the King?" 


TIME-HONOURED CUSTOM 


\ antl War Office 
nh regimental march for 


Tank Corps had receiv.

the Roya 
ed oMeial approach adds yel ap 
other nh le the melodic chain of 
military tradition So many 
the dime honoured customs of sol- 
dievniy have been scrapped sines 
the nenmerable year, 2914, that it 
all the more gratifying lo ac. 
knowledge the  cantinuance of 
service tradition as 
regimental march. 

The oldest regimental mareh ix 
the “British Grenadiers,” going 
back ax if does to the days when 
the Grenadiers were known as the 
First Guards. Very popular, in- 
deed, was this old ditty with’ its 
“tow, raw, row,” refrain, and at 
the present day it is not only the 
ceremonial march of the men who 
once wore the “Cap and Pouch, 
and louped clothes,” but it ia also 
the common property of other 
Fusilier regiments, who in the 
days gone by did duty with the 
hand grenade. 

“The Jocks” 

From a popular point of view, 
the second place must be award- 
ed to “Highland Laddie," which is 
the ceremonial march of the 
“Scots Guards,” as well aa severe 
kilted regiments that are familiar- 
ly known throughout the service 
“The Jocks.” The Lothians 
have for their march an old time 
known as “Dumbarton's Drums,” 
while the Borderers, aptly enough, 
swing by on parade to the air of 
Scott's slirring lyric, “Blue 
Bonnets oer the Border." The 
Cameronians keep time to “With: 
in a Mile o’ Edinburgh Toun,” and 
the Highland Light Infantry put 
their best foot foremost with 
Whistle O'er the Lave O’it. Merry 
times indeed, are these old 
Sacobite airs when they are listen- 
ed to on the march; carrying with 
them they do a flavour of 
xrit and devilry, which factors go 
a long way towards making them 
first favourites even with the sol- 
diers of our own day, 

Many English regiments, too, 
have adopted the old Scottish airs 
as their regimental marches, for 
instance, the Middlesex Regiment 
with their “Lass o’ Gowrie,” the 
Royal West Kents with "A Hun- 
dred Pipers,” and the Lancasters 
with “Corn Rigs,” while such a 
typical regiment as the Cheshires 
still march to the inspiring strains 
of “Wha Wad'na Fecht for 
Charlie"!

Welsh Tunes 

Of the Welsh tunes adopted for 
regimental marches, “Men of Har- 
lech,” “God Bless the Prince of 
Wales,” and the rollicking “Ap 
Sherkin" may be said to comprise 
the list. The last named is the 
march of the Welsh Guards, while 
the first is also a popular march 
with cavalry regiments. With the 
“Trish Guards,” “Connaught Rang- 
ers,” “Faugh-a-Ballaghs,” and 
other regiments of the sister Isle, 
St. Patrick’s Day,” “Garryowen,” 
and “Let Erin Remember,” are 
firat favourites with the “Bhoys” 
who, It Is said, can march to the 
divil, and back again. 

Coming now t the English 
songa used as regimental marches, 
we may cite, “The Dashing White 
Sergeant,” “Jockey to the Falr,’? 
"Come Lassies and Lads,” “D'ye 
ken, John Peal’, ” “Here's to tho 
Maiden" and “Her Bright Smile 
Haunts me Still," ote, ete. Of 
the latter song: there ‘fa a pratty 
Uttle story associnbud with. its 


adoption by tho ILAM.O... When. 


the Corps many years ‘ago devld- 
ad to adopt a regimental march, 
the suggeation was made that 


Florence Nightingale, who really’ 


of 


founded military nursing as it is 
known to-day, should be invited 
to select the tune, This she did 
ina letter stil) in existence, and 
added that) “Her Bright Smile 
Haunts me Still," has always been 
my favourite’; therefore the 
KOAMLC. until quite recently, 
marched past to the tune that 
found so much favour with the 
“Lady of the Lamp." Their march 
past now is “Bonnie Nell.” 

Royal Tank Corps 

The song which is now the 
march of the Royal Tank Corps is 
known as “My Boy Willie,” which 
is taken from Ceell Sharpe’s col- 
lection of Folk Songs. When the 
idea of a regimental march was 
suggested, the officers unanimous- 
ly decided to have a real good old 
English tune, and after many had 
been tried, the final choice fel] on 
“My Boy Willie.” : 
“Dominion troops have now quite 
as great a liking for distinctive 
marches us the British Army. 
Just as we cannot imagine the 
Gordon Highlanders parading to 
any other tune than “Cock 0’ the 
North,” neither can we imagine 
any branch of the Canadian Army 
marching past to anything but the 
“Maple Leaf for Ever.” 

Foreign Ditties 

While it is easy to see why 
‘British folk-songs are so popular 
as ceremonial marches, it is diffi- 
cult to account for the adoption 
of such | foreizn ditties as 
“Milangllo” until quite recently 
the march of the Coldstream 
Guards, and “Ca Fra,” a French 
revolutionary song used by the 
West Yorks. Then there are also 
“Braganza,” the march of the 
“Queen's,” “Romaika” the favour- 
ite tune of the Oxford Kegiment, 
and “La Mandolinata,” which the 
Bedford Boys have marched to for 
half a century. 

Several regiments have two 
marches. | quote for example “The 
Queen’s Own Cameron Highland- 
ers”; the :pipers play the “Pibroch 
O’Donuil Dhu,” while the military 
band, if called upon to play the 
regiment past, play “The March 
of the Cameron Men.” 

In a few regiments what might 
be termed specia] marches bear- 
ing the title of the regiment, have 
been called into service, and there 
ure at least two notable instances 
where even Royalty itself haa 
vied with the bandmaster in ,the 
production of a regimental quick- 
ste§. The late Prince Consort’ is 
credited with a march styled. 
“Prince Alberts,” which was com- 
posed for the Somerset Regiment; 
while H.R.H. Princess Augusta 
was responsible for the march 
‘Windsor" which. haa for many 
years been associated with the 
Worcester Regiment, 

Hong Kong Volunteers 

Of local interest may be men- 
tioned the march of the Hong 
Kong Volunteer Defence Corps.

ly formed band were making 
steady progress and would soon 
be called upon to take their mlace 
on. cdremonial . parades, Mr.

mandant, and suggest that a 
tune (connected. with the Corps 
if any were known) be ddupted ag

aeurched but without success,

pact, and was heard forthe frat 
tine on the occuslon of the Pro- 
sontution of Colours to the Corps

Chow Hsi-chen 

Reuter's cable adds that Gen- 
eral Chow Hsi-chen (Chow Sai-

ship of the Provincial Govern- 
ment of Kweichow, for conspiracy 
against the National Government. 
General Chow sided with the 
Kwangsi faction in the war, it will 
be recalled. 


Swatow Stalemate 


Swatow, Yesterday. 
No further reports of military 
activities in the upper valley ‘of 
the Han River have come to hand.

forces in cast Kwangtung have 
prepared a defence system against 
the projected attack of pro-Canton 
troops under Chiang Kwang-nai and 
Choi Ting-kai, division commanders 
hitherto under General Chan Ming- 
shu, but, as yet, no action has been 
tuken by pro-Canton forces against 
Swatow.

London, Yesterday. 

Though the time of departure of 
the special train taking the Cabinet 
members to Windsor to receive the 
seals of office was not announced, in 
order w avoid scenes not in keeping 
with the dignity of the occasion, 
passengers who were crowding 
around the train at the opposite 
platform at Paddington recognised 
Mr. Ramsay MacDonald and his 
colleagues, all in top hats and 
morning dress. 

Several passengers shouted "Good 
Old Ramsay.”

Those “Resignations” 
Canton, Yesterday. 


~ The” present, authoritiesin- Ogn- 


ton, being supporters of «Markhal’ 
Chiang Kai-shek, are determined to 
dissuade him from resigning the | 
Kuomintang leadership. The other 
day, a coolie, instructed by an un- 
known person, submitted a message 
to a local telegraph office with the 
regulated fees, the message being 
addressed to Chiang Kai-shek at 
Nanking, commending his offer to 
resign. 

On the face of it, there was no- 
thing illegal about it. On a censor 
reading the message, however, the 
tgolie was questioned. 

Sender Unknown 

The coolie explained that he was 
entirely ignorant of the contents of 
the message and that he had aimply 
carried it in order to earn a few 
coppers. The sender of the mes- 
sage did not give the genuine ad- 
dress, it is said. 

On the other hand, all messages 
requesting .Chiang Kai-shek ta re- 
main, purporting to be signed by 
4 public organisation, will be des- 
patched by a sub-committee of the 
Kuomintang, free of charge—Nan 
Chung, Kuo News Service.

LABOUR CABINET AT WINDSOR 


_ SCENES EN ROUTE 


Crowd Shout “Good Old Ramsay ” 


“LOOKS WELL” 


The second Labour Cabinet made a journey to Windsor Castle 
yesterday to receive the seals of office from His Majesty the King. 


journey are narrated. 


Mr. Ramsay MacDonald brought away a cheerful impression of 


Press comments on the new Cabinet are encouraging. 


BONDFIELD 


attire was afforded by Miss 
“Maggie” Bondfield, the first woman 
member of a Cabinet. She wore 
u bright velvet ‘hat, white blouse, 
black costume, and a long black 
coat. She received the warmest re- 
ception from the crowd and shook 
hands with her colleagues in the 
train. 

She wax persuaded to come out 
and pose for a battery of camera 
men. 


The Cabinet members arrived at 
Windsor at eleven o'clock in the 
morning. They took carriages simi- 
lar to the out-going Ministers yes- 
terday with postilions and gootmen. 

The people who were gathered at 
the foot of Castle Hill cheered the 
party, who returned to London an 
hour later.

the Left Wing of the Labourities.

Rails 


MOTOR CAR COMPETITION 

The replacement of old rails on! 
the tramJine running up to the Peak 
has been completed, it was an- 
nounced on dune 7 at the annual 
meeting of Peak Tramways Co., 
Ltd., held in the Hong Kong Hotel. | 

Mr. Henry Humphreys presided. 
He was supported by Mr. J. Scott 
Harston, Mr. R. E. Coxon and Mr.i

Motor Competition 

Turning to the accounts, traffic 
receipts show a falling off of 
$4,681.69 and the net profit a de- 
crease of $1,679.64, as compared 
with the previous year, As regards 
trafic receipts, there is no doubt we 
have been affected to some extent 
by motorcar competition, which 
will probably become more accent- 
uated when May-road is opened up 
tor motor traffic. 

As regards the very big increase 
under the heading “Stations, Lease- 
holds and Buildings,” amounting to 
$246,790.95, 1 would remark that 
neurly the whole of this increase is 
due to sundry items, viz., Upper 
Terminus, Reconstruction Lower 
Terminus, Car Shelter Lower 
Terminus, Engine House, Inland 
Lot 2,306 Bowen-road, and Work- 
shop Lugard-road (formerly shown 
separately), belng included under 
this one heading, at the suggestion 
of the Auditors.

CORRECT POSTAGE 


Sending the “Overland China 
Mail’ Home 


+ 


Thanks to the courtesy of the 
Postmaster General, it is learn- 
eg that a number of people 
have, at various times, “sent o 
considerable number of ‘Over- 
land China Mails’ to addresses 
in the Unite! Kingdom”; and 
that these have been destroyed 
owing to:— 

(a) insufficient 
paid, 

(b) names of senders not on 
wrappers. 

Each copy of the “Overland” 
weighs over four ounces. The 
correct postage to the United 
Kingdom, , therefore, is four 
cents per copy (ie, up to 
eight ounces). 

Bear in mind the correct 
weight (and the ratr of post- 
age it incurs) and, preferably, 
put the name and address of 
sender on back, Best of all, 
send in your order to the 
“Overland China Mail” with 
the name and addres: of ad- 
dressee and everything will be 
done correctly for you. 


postage pre-

CANADIAN FUR 


Bearers Go To Ten 
Countries 


MontreaL—Canadian ranch bred fur 
bearing animals found their way dur- 
ing the past year to ten foreign 
countries for use aB breeding stock, 
necording to a tabulation of ship- 
mens of fur bearers for export kept 
by the Canadian National Express. 

Canadian National Express was ¢n- 
trusted with crates for delivery to 
England, Germany, Scotland, France, 
Sweden, Finland, Holland, Switzer- 
land, Belgium and Czecho-Slavakia, 
These shipments originated in Prince 
Edward Island, Ontario, Manitoba and 
Alberta. 

Black and silver foxes predominat- 
ed in the export list, but ~muskrats, 
raccoons and mink were also handl- 
ed. Altogether Canada exported 
about one thousand fur bearers in 
1928, a figure almost double that of 
1927.

Hong Kong Tug And 
Lighter Co. 


An extraordinary general mecting of 
shareholders of the Hong Kong ‘Tug 
and Lighter Co., Ltd, was held on 
June 7.

posed that the Company be wound up 
voluntarily. Mr. Li Tsc-fong seconded 
and thia was carried. ' *

were appointed liquidators.

BRITISH GOLF 


Not for many years has British 
golf been So uplifted as ft has beon 
during the Jast week (says a wril- 
in n Home paper of May 4), and 
new note of hope will be carried 
Moortown to Muirfield. 
Sinee the war we have had the plea- 
sure of 
Championships, and the portents are 
that Muirfield will provide another 
great and enthralling page to add to 
the history of the game. A 
British victory worthily won against 
the cream—-with one exceplion—of 
vur overseas challengers is a con- 
summation greatly to be desired, and 
even our American friends, while de- 
termined to carry the trophy on an- 
other trip across the Atlantic, would 
probably agree that in the interests 
of international rivalry thut would be 
a result that would in the end make 
for even greater interest in the mect- 
ings between the twe countries. 
Such a possibility seems less remote 
than it) did ten days ago. Surely 
tha! finely-won victory in the Ryder 
Cup must be of sume inoral value 
when the curtain lifts on Monday. 
The Americans had no excuse, and 
like the good sportsmen they are, 
offered none, They came brimful of 
confidence for their task after a win- 
ler of preparation; every man wax 
fighting fit; they knew the course as 
well as did their opponents; the 
weather was, on the whole, excellent, 
and they played golf that was real- 


ty geod, worthy of themselves and of 
the arcasion.

The Value of the Victory 

That the British team could rise 
to even greater heights emphasises 
the value of their victory. A win 
over indifferent. opponeuts would have 
been no success at all. A week be- 
fore the match, Duncan wrote— 
“Honestly, | think we can beat the 
Americans if we put our backs inte 
th job.” They did, fighting with a 
rare spirit. of determination that 
was in’ the best British traditions. 
That same determination should not 
elude them now. In the past seven 
or cight years the sweeping flood of 
American success has Jed to frequent 
analysis of the play of our leading 
golfers. Firsp of all in the year 
that Hagen won at Sandwich we 
were most impressed with their con- 
sistent ability in rolling two shots 
into one in the vicinity of the flag: 
then the shol up to the green as 
played by Jones sevined to be more 
necurate than anything we coukl pro- 
duce: and all the time putting has 
been our bane.

Selectors' Nightmare 
While professional footballers ara 
haggling over their summer wages, 
and the Arsenal ‘are turning down, 
once and for all, the proposition that 
their ground should be used in the 
close season as a_ dirt-track, the 
selectors are faced wtih a serious 
problem. They have to choose a_re- 
presentative team to tour the Con- 
tInent, and their task is no easy 
one. Already a team has to go to 
South Africa and another to Canada, 
ond many professionals take on 
other jobs, as cricket; so a shortage 
of talent may be expected. Club 
managers are not ai] too keen on 
their men touring during the sum- 
mer, for, although the selection adds 
to a player’s market value, staleness 
is a problem always to be contend- 
ed with. Th’s is naturally an im- 
portant point to be considered if the 
player is to be retained. and the 
danger of it setting in is always pre- 
sent if a man plays summer and 


winter.

Lawn Tennis 
Staniey N. 
Home paper: 


Doust writes in a 
There ix a gleam of 
hape that Mrs. L. A. Godfree, the 
ex-woman lawn tennis champion, 
may after all play in the singles at 
Wimbledon. Mrs. Godfree acting on 
the imstructions of her medical ad- 
viser, has abandoned the idea of 
playing in any of the singles pre- 
ceding Wimbledon. The strain would 
be too great and would probably 
make her unfit even to play in the 
doubles. But Mrs. Godfree informed 
me that if she feels perfectly well 
by the lime the championships begin 
om June 26 she might be tempted to 
Play in the singles. Mrs. Godfree 
more than any other Brilish player 
has a very good chance of beating 
Miss Helen Wills and Senorita de 
Alvarez. She first won the Utle at 
Wimbledon in 1924 when she beat 
Miss Wills in a very close match, 
after losing the first set and being 
4 games to 1 and 40—14 down in 
the second set. In 1926 she beat 
Senorita de Alvarez in the final, 
Miss Wills confessed ta me last year 
that she feared Mrs. Godfree most 
of all. His therefore hoped that 
the latter will be “perfectly fit’ by 
June for her own sake and for 
the sake of the prestige of British 
lawn tennis. :

Viscount Finlay’s death recalls the 
circumstances in which Sir Edward 
Clarke, Q.C., refused the Solicitor- 
Generalship in Lord Salisbury's Ad- 
ministration. A previous — Liberal 
Government had abrogated the privi- 
jege which the law officer: en- 
juyed of combining p-ivate. practice 
with official dutics, Sir Edward 
made it a condition of his accepting 
the position that the privilege shauld 
be restored. Lord Salisbury was in- 
disposed to make the change, and 
Mr. Finlay, Q.C., became “Mr. Soli- 
citor.” Sir Edward Clarke makes 
generous allusion to the matter in 
his autobiography. “The country lost 
nothing by my refusal of the office. 
It gained the services of one of the 
ablest men [ have ever known, a 
sound lawyer, shrewd,in judgment 
and clear in argument, of instinctive 
honesty in purpose and in deed.” 

The marriage of Miss Ruth Adair 
Alexander of Yenching to Mr, Walter 
Sterling Price of the American Lega- 
tion was solemnised at St. Joseph's 
Church,, Peking, Father O‘CUonnell 
officiating. Miss Nita Cherry of 
Yenching acted as bridesmaid and 
Mr. Alvin G. Bandy, of the legation, 
as best man. The bride wore a 
gown of pale yellow georgette over 
white satin, with a tulle veil and 
carried a sheaf of Easter lilies. 
Miss Cherry wore a frock of blue 
charmeuse with a metallic lace hat 
and carried yellow roses. The bride, 
who is the daughter of Mr, and Mrs. 
Robert S. Alexander of Blue Mound, 
Kansas, has made her home in China 
for-the past eight months. She is a 
graduate of the law school of New 
York University. Mr. Price recently 
became associated with the chancery 
of the American Legation. He is a 
former resident of Denver, Colorado.

were regarded as leing well on the way

yesterday at. Happy Valley.

style, and if was soon apparent that 
they would seriously extend last year’s 
champions. For the home team, only 
Basa's rink held its own, but the mar- 
gin here of 13 shots was not sufficient 
to cover the deficit on the other two 
rinks

ahots on one rink, defeated the Taikoo 
R.C, rather easily.

this season, by trouncing their neigh- 
bours, the Cricket Club by 56 shots.

FOOTBALL 


The Council’s Swan 
Song 


NEW BODY TO FUNCTION 


On Tuesday last, at their New 
Hendquartera on the fourth floor of 
the French Bank offices, Quéen’s Road, 
the Council of the Hong Kong Football 
Association held their last meeting for 
the year prior to the annual General 
meeting in June 28. 

Tho chicf business was the proposed 
alterations to the Rules, following the 
winding up of the Hong Kone Amateur 
Football League, which latter body 
will, after their annual general mect- 
ing on Tuesday next June 1] ceage 
to function, the H.K-F.A. Council, 
which will be clected on June 28 next, 
taking over the whole contro] of Hong 
Kong football in all its branches in 
future. 


A welcome reappearance at the meet- 
ing, after protracted iJlness owing to 
an air smash, was Captain Austin, 
M.M., M.C., R.A 

These alterations were practical and 
necessary, and, with few amendments, 
were unanimously passed. 

At. the conclusion of the business, 
Mr. Robert Hall, Chairman of the 
Council, provided a pleasant surprise, 
hy asking Captain Austin to accept on 
behalf of his confreres on the Council 
and the football public generally of 
Hong Kong, two handsome silver 
souvenirs for his wife and himself, in 
recognition of Captain Austin’s untir- 
ing efforts in the past in reorganising 
the rules of the local Association, and 
of his assistance generally to local 
football. Mr. Hall wished him good 
luck in his voyage Home. 

Captain Austin, bearing signs of his 
recent serious accident. thanked the 
Chairman and all connected with the 
occasion, and said he had always taken 
a keen pleasure in all the activities of 
local football world. 

The mecting then adjourned fully 
prepared for the serious business of the 
annual general meeting. 

The annual report, which this year 
js an extremely geod one, is in the 
hands of the auditors. The Clubs have 
notice of all the recommendations, pro- 
posed by the Council and also by the 
individual Clubs. 

The retiring Council and particular- 
ly the sub-committees of the retiring 
Council have had a particularly busy 
season, and the incoming Council, 
which we hope will contain many new 
members, should start the scason 
1929-80 under very happy auspices. 

Football oa a winter sport in the 
Colony has a great future and the ex- 
pansion of the individual Clubs, to men- 
tion only, the Recrcio, Kowloon, South 
China, and the Chinese Athletic in the 
past few years is indicative of great 
possibilities.

LAWN TENNIS 


Rain Spoils League 
Programme 


GOOD FOR COURTS 

A welcome bgeak in the long 
spell of dry weather was respon- 
sible for the postponement of all 
the tennis ‘League fixtures arrang- 
ed for yesterday. 

Apart from the point of view 
that the Colony needed the rain, 
and one would wish that At had 
poured, the “wetting” that the 
sun-baked courts have just receiv- 
ed should help to remove the 
brown patches which have become 
rather prominent on some of them. 

“Will the Hong Kong’ Cricket 
Club carry off all three champion- 
ships?” This question is being 
generally asked in tennis circles at 
the moment. The Club have ac- 
counted for a few of the strongest 
sides in the “A” League and are 
expected to finish their fixtures with- 
out a defeat, but the Chinese R.C. 
and the Club de Recreio will not be 
easily overcome in the “B" and “C” 
divisions, respectively.

THE “TAI FLEET 


Messrs. Barber §S.S. Lines, New 
York, up to a number of years ago 
had o fleet of steamers on the New 
York Far Eastern trade which came 
out via the Panama Cunal and re- 
turned to New York vin Suez In 
recent years Messrs. Barber had a 
controlling interest in u number of 
U.S. Shipping Board vessels, but 
these have been disposed of to pri- 
vate interests, With the increasing 
demand for speed it was difficult to 
find a suitable tonnage to place on 
the run, but finally negotiations were 
concluded with Messrs. Wm. Wilhelm- 
sen, Oslo, the well-known Norwegian 
Owner to charter 5 new motor ves- 
sels. These vessels at the time had 
only just been ordered, and in fact 
the first vessel, the “Tai Yong,” was 
only delivered in March this year. 

It is interesting to note that the 
vessels have been given Chinese 
names and are as follows:— 

“Tai Ping,” 
“Tai Ping Shan,” 
“Tai Shan,” 
“Tai Yang,” 
“Tai Yin.” 

They are all designed te carry 
4,600 tons deadweight for cargo with 
a bale capacity of 14,000 tons of 40 
cubie feet at a speed of 16 knots 
when fully laden representing the 
modern -.conception of high speed 
cargo liners.

as a temporary measure to 
approve..of, every application 
for the sinking and re-opening 
of wells, except in cases where

alangered, the water derived 
from auch sources to be utilis-

the. purpose of drinking and 

for the preparation of food.”

Shelter Deck Type 


The vessels are of the shelter-deck 
type, and the double bottom, which 
is 3.9 feet high, extends the whole 
length of the ship and is utilised for 
the carriage of fuel oil, seven tanks 
being provided. 

Accommodation for further fuel is 
arranged in tanks between and at 
the sides of the tunnels, a deck be- 
ing built over them aft of the engine 
room, 

Forward of the engine reom are 
three cargo holds, and two aft, 
‘tween decks, 9 feet high, being ar- 
ranged in the three forward holds. 

A deep tank with a capacity of 
1,400 tons w.b., which has a coffer- 
dom in the centre diving tank, is 
situated in No, 3 lower hold. These 
tanks will be fitted with heating 
coils and will be available for the 
carriage of oil in bulk. In addition 
three tanks between the tunnels with 
a total capacity of 240 tons will be 
fitted with heating coils ond when 
necessary can be utilised for the car- 
riage of oil in bulk. 

Refrigerated space has also been 
installed and is situated in the 
"tween deck of No. 4 hold. The total 
insulated space in each vesse) will be 
29,948 cu. ft. divided into 4 com- 
partments to accommodate perishable 
cargo. <A special refrigerating en- 
gineer will accompany each vessel. 

In all about 1,658 tons of fuel can 
be carried, detracting, as will be 
seen, very little indeed from the 
cargo space, ond as the daily fuel 
consumption at full speed when laden 
is in the neighbourhood of 24 tons 
for all purposes, these relatively high 
speed cargo liners have a radius, of 
action of nearly 24,000 miles without 
rebunkering. They are naturally able 
to take on fuel in any part on their 
route where it is cheapest, and even 
for their longest trades it is usually 
only necessary to bunker once for 
the round trip.

cabins, in addition to which there is 
an owner’s suite, comprising a sleep- 
ing cabin, sitting room and bath 
room. This suite adjoins ‘the Cap- 
tain’s day room and sleeping room 
on the lower bridge deck,. where is 
also one of the two berth cabins. 
Below are the saloon and four two 
berth cabins, with a hospital, whilst 
a smoking room is provided on the 
upper bridge. The Captain's quarters 
and the owner’s suite, as well as 
both saloons are in: polished 
mahogany with mahogany furniture, 
whilst the remaining passenger 
cabins are finished in white enamel 
and have mahogany furniture. 

The officers and engincers are 
berthed around the ;engine room 
casing, with a mess rogm at the for- 
ward end and. a‘ galley hft, whilst the 
deck staff and the motor men. have 
two berth cabins which are located 
at .the after end of: thd “vessel. ;

in 
their 


been 
Police 


We have alivays 

supporters of the 
the legitimate exercise of 
functions, but when they des- 
cend to staging a circus 
like the one they staged outside the 
entrance of 151 € Bubbling Well in 
the small hours of fas. Sunday 
morning, wasting their own time 
and the time of a number of harm- 
less, quiet, and law-abiding persons, 
we are afraid that if this is the 
spirit which the Police are going to 
adopt towards folks who are mind- 
ing their own business that we, as 
a public mirror of comman ser.se in 
the give and take of life here, may 
have to adopt a similarly critical at- 
titude towards the Police—and the 
doing of that would work much 
more harm than their recent petti- 
fogging action can possibly work 
good, 

Let the Police remember an in- 
cident in history when a world 
figure put the telescope to his blind 
eye! There is virtue in the blind 
eye at times, and the whole circum- 
stances connected with 151 C Bub- 
bling Well are an example for the 
exercise of this common sense at- 
tribute of men of the world, and the 
Police know this as well as we do. 

Roulette 


And what are the circumstances? 
Simply these: —At 151 € Bubbling 
Well there is alleged to be a private 
flu owned by citizens of Mexico 
where a roulette wheel runs for the 
amusement of such friends and ac- 
yuaintanees of the proprietor as he 
chooses to invite to and allow ne- 
cosas to his) promises. 

The spinning of a roulette 
wheel is mot a wicked or 8 
hwrong action and is based upon the | 
most meticulous regard for private 
honour and fair dealing all round 

The conduct of the proceedings 
yalso ix based upon a most meticul- 
uns regard for the usages of polite 
society, and anyone contravening 
‘th in the slightest degree finds | 
himself denied any further privi- 
leges of entree to this cirele of his 
friends and acquaintances. 

There is nothing of any sort or 
description connected with the con- 
duet af 15) © Bubbling Well which 
ean be taken exception to by any- 
one who has been brought up with 
the instincts of a gentleman, and 
the harrying and pettifogging vic- 
timisation of this place has been in- 
stituted by those who are lack- 
ing in certain instincte. This is 


CIRCUS — 


BLIND .EYE 


CRITICISED 


Me 


(“N. C. D. News, May 27, 1929.) 

They descend in force upon the 
place as if it was a nest of armed 
robbers and kidnappers and crimin- 
als, their Acting-Commissioner 
being accompanied by superintend- 
ants, inspectors, sergeants, and con- 
stables armed to the teeth, with 
such war-like atcessories as barbed 
wire entanglements, searchlights, 
machine guna, revolvers, “Red 
Maria's,” commissariat waggons, 
plus a “Hong List” and a supply 
of Ever-sharp pencils! 

How ludicrous, and how utterly 
futile and silly! 

Ridiculous Stunt 

Their quarry was a number of in- 
offensive ladies and gentlemen in 
evening dress who had been danc- 
ing and=possibly losing or winning 
some dollars on the spinning of an 
ivory bail. These persons were 
harming or annoying—NO-ONE, 
no-one at all, and the Police must 
needs stage this ridiculous stunt 
which was the most utter waste of 
time and effort for everyone con- 
cerned. 
Technically, they were not even 
sure that the individuals whose 
names and addresses they demand- 
ed had even attended a roulette 
wheel, or had even staked upon it. 
In short, if there was any con- 
travention of ordinary usage, it was 
on the part of the Police and not 
on the part of the  Pub- 
lic on this occasion, in so far as 
the Police deliberately molested the 
Pubtic and detained them upon 
their lawful occasions against their 
will. 
The Police had no excuse for 
erecting their silly barbed wire en- 
tanglements which was more the 
act of a pack of mischievous minded 
school boys, then the act of respon- 
sible authority jn such clreum- 
stances. 


Awesome Spectacle 


To the men present it appeared as 
what it should have done, namely, a 
huge joke,--but to many of the 
women there whose sense of 
humour is not masculine, it anpear- 
ed an awesome spectacle, in addi- 
tion to un annoying thing by -ar- 
ing their gowns and cloaks. 

If the Police had no actual busi- 
ness in hand last Saturday even- 
ing such as the detection or preven- 
tion of real crime, then it would 
have Leen better for them to have 
seized upon their leisure for their 
own yest and recreation, instead 
of manufacturing an occasion for 


nol merely our own opinion but is 
supported by that of many high 
naval, military, and civil officers 
yholding the commissions of H.M. 
The King. The President of the 
U.S.A, and otner foreign Govern- 
ments, We say this advisedly. 
Not Jmproper 

No one considers it improper Lo 
attend the gaming tables at Monte 
Carlo, at Deauville, and many 
other well known) places, or 
the Puri-mutuel counters of 
the Shanghai Race Club in 
Bubbling Well-road, if we 
need an example very close at hand. 
Nor is it improper to do so. 


Why is it wicked to attend an 
exceedingly well conducted roulette 
table a little further up the strect 
to which access is strictly limited, 
and where guests are shroffed over 
with the greatest care? 


It is not wicked of course, and 
only fools will presume to dictate 
as to the way individuals choose 
to spend, their time when they are 
neither harming nor annoying any- 
one else in the whole wide world. 

Let us analyse the actual incident 
of last week-end. 

The Police are well aware that 
many ladies and gentlemen are in 
the habit of resorting to 151 C Bub- 
*|bling Well, and they are also well 
aware that it is so quietly and res- 
pectably conducted that they have 
never had to station a sinyle con- 
stable in its vicinity for the 
preservation of what is called “Law 
and Order.” 

They also know that the ladies 


the unnecessary display of the force 
that is rightly at their disposal in 
their continual warfare with the 
criminal population. 

But to bring out all this para- 
phernalia simply to embarrass 
a number of inoffensive ladies and 
gentlemen on a Saturday evening's 
legitimate amusement is an instance 
of “swank” and “throwing — their 
weight about” on the part of the 
high command that makes it look 
extremely silly to common sense 
men of the world. 

Police Antics 

Our advice to those who enjoy 
the diversion of roulette ‘s not to 
allow themselves to be in any way 
incommoded by police antics. but 
to remember they are doing nothing 
they have the least reason to be 
ashamed of, and are harming no 
one. Do not allow yourselves to 
feel angry with the Police when 
the spirit moves them to be public- 
ly silly, and alweys remember they 
are just as liable to the faults and 
failings of our common human 
nature as everyone else. 

After the Police have staged one 
or two more circuses of this nature, 
they are sure to come to their senses 


‘And alnce it is so; we ‘nope the 


Pilico will likewise “recognise thia|_ 


aspect in relation . te ‘Inoffensive 
people who choose to amuse them- 
selves in this or that way without 
harming anyone else. 

Live and Let Live 

“Live and fet live’. is the spirit 
we would invite the Police to culti- 
vate in regard to this. passing 
excitement about “gambling.” 

Let them send men to sec that 
other roulette wheels than those at 
151 C Bubbling Well are run on the 
square, for there is no question 
whatever as to the strict and 
meticulous propriety of this parti- 
cular one. 

And, if they are satisfied timt 
things arc straight and on tho 
square, then let them adopt the 
Nelson attitude and turn a blind 
eye to the foibles of human nature 
so Jong as everything is conducted 
in a quiet and orderly manner. 

And if they adopt this common 
sense attitude, other people are only 
too ready to adopt a common sense 
attitude towards the Force, on such 
occasions ag human nature slips up 
and does things it is sorry for after- 


wards. 


One for the Council! 


As for the Council, a body of men 
who can dip their hands deep into 
public funds and pay Mr. A. C. 
Clear Tis. 61,000 for eighteen 
months work in the name of 
“Economy”,—being Tis. 2,000 a

Hypocrisy and Cant. 

We say that public morality is 
best forwarded by recognising cer- 
tain basic facts about human nature, 
one of which is that many human 
beings like to gamble in one way or 
another, and that to harry and 
worry a well conducted roulette 
wheel which is une of the fairest and 
best forms of gambling in the 
world, and, at the same time, to 
allow organised public gambling on 
the pari-mutuel system at the Race 
clubs, is an act of hypocrisy, of 
cant, and of civic dishonesty. 

It is unfair and discriminative 
on the face of H, and conduces to 
teach the people murky minded 
methods of thinking. 

A man conducting a roulette 
wheel in a careful and proper man- 
ner is entitled to protection, and not 
victimisation, which, hitherto, has 
been tne portion of 151 C,—and it 
is high time this mean spirited and 
meanly conceived victimisation de- 
finitely ceased. 

Jt is in the interests of real 
public morality that it should cease, 
and we urge the exercise of -human 
common sense in relation to it— 
he Showdown, June 1. 


and grasp the great fact that their 
essential business is not concerned 
with harrying their friends, but 
with chasing our common enemies, 
the criminal classes.

Ye regarded as a finanuvial invest-

ty do

jag money by falxe pretences,

Let me conclude these rambling. 
referencees to H.R.H. and his Can-; 
adian ranch with another little 
story. 

In front of the unpretentious 
ranch cottage, with its wide veran- 
da, extends an enclosure of 
smoothly-cut grass. One evening 
the Prince and his party with a 
few friend were slitting on the 
steps. 

“You know,” the Prince remark- 
ed, “I tove this place.” 

He looked around to the low foot- 
hills that mark his land, and far- 
ther across to the peaks of the Roc- 
kies, 

“But it cannot compare with your 
other places in the Old Country,” 
someone commented. 

“Ah, but they are not mine as 
this place is,” the Prince replied. 
“This is my home. And when I 
marry I hope to bring my wife here 
for our honeymoon.” 

He did not say “if 1 get married,” 
but “when I get married.”

M. C. L. FAIR 

The Women’s Guild and the 
Ministering Children’s League 
have decided to hold a Fair this 
year (instead of a combined Fete 
and Sale of Work) on the Murray 
Parade ground, by kind permis- 
sion of Lt.-Col. Comyn, D.S.0., 
Q.C., the 2nd Battalion King’s 
Own Scottish Borderers. 

The Fair will take place on 
Friday, November 1, and Satur- 
day November 2, and “will be 
run on the ines of an English 
Fair. 

It is hoped to have many 
amusements for children as well |, 
ag adults. There will be a cabaret 
show and, if possible, a dancing 
floor. / 

Further particulars will be 
given in the “Sunday Herald” 
later.

A Piteous water supplied to 
Scene the people in the 
vicinity of Yaumati 

and Mongkok has been cut off 
for practically the whole day 
(bar seven hours) the 
Chinese living there have great 
difficulty in obtaining even a drop 
of water. As is common know- 
ledge the queues at every street- 
fountain are of tremendous 
length, and the person whose 
buckets are at the very end of the 
line has, needless to say, an un- 
enviable experience. After a 
woman has been waiting her 
turn very patiently, and perhaps 
is the fourth one from the foun- 
tain, having moved up step by 
| step, imagine her feelings when 
she learns that the water has 
been turned off, just at the mo- 
ment when she is most likely to 
‘get a drop! Horrible and cruel! 
| Then, because she happened to be 
, one of the unfortunate ones, she 
| has not a drop of water—at least 
there is none to get from the 
official source. It may be said 
| that she ought to have taken her 
place in the queue earlier so as 
to be in time before the mains 
were cut off. But there are 
many others who are desirous for 
a drop and they also “bag” their 
place early—in fact, directly the 
water has ceased to be supplied 
buckets are shored in their places 
ready for the next day’s supply.

.Nullah Water cannot be de- 
nied, when one 

sees the huge crowd every day— 
especially in the -afternoon—who 
gather water from the nullah 
that runs alongside Waterloo 
Road. The nullah is practically 
dry as it is, and the thirsty" 
crowd lower themselves by means 
of ropes, and thus with the help 
of a dipper, gradually fill their 
buckets, which are in turn hoisted 
on to the roadway, But even the 
nullah is as, if not more crowded 
than, the street-fountain area. 
Every single person in Yaumati 
seems, to congregate there to 
| get water. Thus it will be seen 
that for hundreds it is’ very dif- 
ficult to get any water at all.

It was stated re- 
cently in the 
papers that 
H.E. the 
ernor, when asked if well water 
should be brought into use, 
thought this was not desir- 
able, because of the fear of 
water-borne diseases. True! 
But does not the Government 
think that nullah water may con- 
tain more germs of typhoid and 
cholera than well water? Hong 
Kong long ago had many wells—. 
in fact, a large percentage of the 
European residents had each a 
well of their own, and then they 
did not experience such a crisis 
as is prevailing to-dav. The 
latest. reports concerning the 
water famine are to the effect 
that sanction has been given for 
certain wells to be opened. So 
that in svite of the objection 
originally raised, the Government 
has given way to a certain ex- 
tent.

One may not be 
Rendezvous wrong in saying 
For _that the most 
Robbers popular — rendez- 
vous for robbers 
on thé mainland is Gascogine- 
road in the vicinity of King's 
Park. At night when all is quiet, 
Gascognie-road presents an 
amiable “theatre” in which the 
robbers and their victim are the 
chief characters. The road leads 
from Yaumati to Hunghom, and 
must on occasions, (we will not 
say always) reward the evil-doer 
with a fine harvest, and, in- 
cidentally, encourage — further 
exploits of a similar kind. There 
is not a month in which someone 
returning from Yaumati to Hung- 
hom or vice versa, is not set upon 
by robbers who usually number 
three. In many cases violence is 
used with drastic results—the 
victims have been known to have 
been stabbed, if the robbers are 
not satisfied with what they have 
stolen. In view of the fact that 
this place is so often a rendezvous 
for robbers—and will continue so, 
for that matter—steps should be 
taken for the safety of the public. 
The Police should place more con- 
stables on beat-duty along the 
whole length of the road, more 
particularly opposite King’s 
Park. 


(Does any old re- 

Dockyard sident remember 
Engineer Mr. W. G. Mogg 
of 1891 as he then was)? 
In 1891, he came 

out to the Royal Naval Dockyard, 
Hong Kong, as Engineer. He had 


been here three years when he|’ 


was promoted Chief Engineer. 
Engineer Rear-Admiral William 
George Mogg, C.B., (as he be- 
came), was found dead on Stokes 
Bay Beach on May 1. A verdict 
of “Suicide while of unsound 
mind” was returned at an inquest 
next day. He was 68 and had 
served on the active list of 
the Royal Navy for over 37 
years. He had been Mayor of 
Gosport, and had held other posi- 
tions in public life. The son of 
Mr. W. H. Mogg, he became an 
assistant engineer, R.N., on April 
1, 1882, In June, 1887, he was 
promoted to Engineer. As. Chief 
Engineer he served ‘in the cruiser 
“Sirius,” and while in her was 
made staff Engineer, in 1898. He 
afterwards went to Australia as 


Many have been scen wandering” 


a goodly lond ; of humnn. 


Staff Engineer of the “Royal 
Arthur,” flagship of Rear- 
‘Admiral Sir Lewis Beaumont, and 
during ,his service there he be- 
came an Engineer Commander, in 
October, 1902. Other vessela in 
which he served included the 
battleship “Renown,” “Repulse” 
and “Hood,” in reserve at home. 
In April, 1910, he joined the staff 
at Devonport, where-he served 
until promoted -to Engineer Cap- 
tain in July, 1911. He then re- 
sumed sea’ duty as Senior En- 
gineer ‘Officer on.the staff of the 
Renr-Admiral Commanding. Third 
Cruiser Squadron, and from 
November, 1912, held a similar 
position with the Reat-Admiral in 
the Home Fleets, Devonport. In 
November, 1913, he was promoted 
td Engineer Rear-Admiral, and in 
July, 1916, was appointed for duty 
on the staff of Admiral the Hon. 
Sir Stanley Colville, Conimunder- 
in-Ohief at Portsmouth, where ho 
served for threo yenrs, rotlring in 
1919: ‘tn the New Yor, Honours 
of. 1919 he was gazetted CB.’

HONG KONG, SUNDAY, JUNE 9, 1929. 


Concentrate On A Municipal Council 
. ! 


‘T cannot be known for some time what effect the change of 
Government at ‘Home will have on the direction of affairs in the 
Crown Colonies. There are undoubtedly measures that would be 
encouraged by a Labour regime that would be cold-shouldered by a 
Conservative Administration. But naturally the’first step would be 
to influence the Government of the Colony to view certain measures 


‘| Colony always going to permit itself to be “caught napping?” 


‘}ment.. 


sympathetically in the greater hope of securing eventually the 
approval ‘of Downing Street, 8 : 

With a Labour Government in power the time seems ripe for 
the local community concentrating on the creation of a Municipal 
Council. “Taxation without representation” is and has always been 
one of the main planks of the Labour Party. Were its leaders now 
in power to enquire into the position in this Colony they would be 
positively amazed and, if they were truly honest, they would marvel 
at the exemplary patience that has been shown by this very cosmo- 
politan community. 

The increase in the population both on the Island and on the 
mainland during the past few years—particularly, of course on the 
latter—has been such that Government Departments cannot cope 
with the vast additional work unaided. Their labours must require 
the natural complement of a Municipal Council. There are phases 
of public service innumerable that could be cited to demonstrate how 
much more efficient could the work be performed by a Municipality. 
And ‘there are glaring examples of work done by Governments that 
a Municipality would be ashamed of “for ever and a day.” Con- 
tinuously there are complaints of overlapping. Always are there 
protests against a system under which one Department is totally 
unaware of the policy and programme of another’Department on the 
same job of work. It might be stretching the point unduly to say 
that some Departments are not spending but wasting Departments 
__-but it is no part of our case in favour of the creation of a Muni- 
cipality to indulge in needless and, mayhap, senseless exaggerations, 
Our case can rest on a surer foundation than that.: ; 

Meanwhile, as a preliminary stage to the actual formation of 
a Municipal Council, cannot the leaders of the various communitiés 
and districts seriously examine the suggestion originally advanced 
in the “Sunday Herald” about a couple of years ago in regard to the 
creation of a Central Council. It may be remembered that the idea 
was advanced that representatives of the existing Ratepayers’ 
‘Association—Kowloon, the Peak, and Mid-Level—should form the 
‘nucleus of such a Central Council and co-opt representatives of those 
districts unprovided so far with a Ratepayers’ Association. On 


| matters of vital importance to the whole Colony—and not merely to 


one specified area—the Central Couneil could make representations 
to the Government and would carry the responsibility of speaking 
on behalf of a united community. att 2 ae

British “ Enterprise ” 

Issued monthly the “Overscas 
Bulletin” of the British Manuf1e- 
turers’ Section, Society of Motor 
Manufacturers and =‘ Traders, 
Limjted, is performing excellent 
service for trade at Home and 
overseas. Thus, the latest issue 
to hand speaks in glowing terms 
of the exploits of Major Segrave 
and Captain Campbell, concliding 
on this very fine note: “British 
manufacturers have challenged 
the world, and in doing so have 
inspired again that confidence in 
the excellence of their own pro- 
ducts which is the life-blood of 
a vigorous and virile nation.” 
That is excellent. But, on another 
page our attention is attracted 
by the heading “Praise from 
China.” And this is what we 
read: “Some time ago Guy 
Motors, Ltd., shipped ten 26- 
seater ’buses for use by the Hong 
Kong Tramways. Reports receiv- 
ed show that: these vehicles are 
giving excellent service in every 
way, and are meeting a long felt 
need in speeding up the local 
transport services.” — How long 
will it take responsible ‘people at 
Home—manufacturers, publicists, 
and politicians—to learn ‘that 
Hong Kong is’ not China any 
more than China is Hong Kong, 
It appears that Empire geography 
is badly neglected in commercial 
circles at Home, but if they do 
not know their geography how 
can they possibly be expected to 
know and Lo meet local trade re- 
quirements? 

“Communique to Press” 

We were agreeably surprised 
to receive on Wednesday a com- 
munication from the Colonial 
Secretary headed “Communique 
to Press.” At last, we thought, 
the value of the Press as a 
medium between the Government 
and the public had: been realised, 
and the hope was. mentally ex- 
pressed that the precedent thus 
set would be followed very often 
in the future. On _ reading 
through the: communique, how- 
ever, we were rudely deceived. It 
is an intimation that “the sum of 
$1,500 has generously been 
donated by Mr. Lo Cheung-shiu, 
to endow a_ scholarship at 
Queen’s College in aid of any pro- 
-mising scholar who may find him- 
self unable to pursue his studies 
through lack of funds, the 
scholarship wilt be called the 
‘Sheung Hing Scholarship’ in 
memory of the donor’s wife.”— 
What stale news to furnish the 
basis of an official communique!

Sir, — I consider it a very 
fortunate thing for the officials of 
the Hong Kong Government to 
escape’the usual censures from the 
pubic whenever anything goes 
wrong in any department. 

The Hong Kong public, including, 
I am sorry to say, the editors of 
the local papers, are very loth to 
pass any comment on what the 
Government does, except at times to 
call their attention to certain 
irregularities and there the matter 
ends and fa never revived. 

The famous Carvalho Yeo case is 
still fresh in the minds of all the 
people of Hong Kong and it seems 
a thousand pities that not one of the 
unofficial members of our Legisla- 
tive Assombly has had the courage 
to ask the Couneil for any informa- 
tion regarding the case in guesion, 
thereby leaving the tax-payer to 
oarry on in paying excessive taxa- 
tion through no fault of their own, 
but simply because the Government 
must find money to meet its 
obligations. : 

Now, we ail know that this for- 
gery case has cost the Government 
(and therefore the tax-payer) a 
considerable amount of expense to 2 
bring the culprit to “book” and to; THEFTS FROM CLUBS’ 
obtain whatever redress they can 
from the Hong Kong & Shanghal 
Bank. 

Will some membef of the Legisla- 
tive Council ask the following 
questions at the next meeting? 

1. When Yeo was committed for 
trial in the High Court, were not 
the following then officials of the 
Government qualified and privileged 
to conduct the case for the prosecu~ 
tion in that Court, namely:— 

(a) The Hon. Mr. C.-G, Alabaster, 
K.C., Acting Attorney-General. 

(b) Mr. Somerset Fitzroy, Assist- 

ant Attorney-General. 

Mr, T. M. Hazlerigg, Treasury 

Solicitor. 

Mr. H. K. Holmes, 

Solicitor. 

Mr. L. R. Andrewes, 

ant Crown Solicitor. 

Mr. E. L. Agassiz, 

and Official Reserver. 

Mr. ©. D. Melbourne, Registrar 

and Barrister-at-law. 

(h) Lt.-Col. F, Eaves, Land Officer 
and Barrister-at-law. 

2: If the answer, is in the 
affirmative, will the Government say 
whether it considered these profes- 
sional men to be incompetent to 
conduct the case for the Crown. 

3. Hf the answer to question (2) 
is in the negative, what is the 
necessity of employing outside legal 
assistance for the case. 

4, What is the total expenditure 
in 
connection with the case and from 
what “funda” were these payments 
made. 

5. In answering question (4) 
will the Government give full details 
of the following obvious expendi- 
ture, viz:— 

(a) Total expenditure incurred by 
the Treasury Department in 
its investigations including the 
expenses incurred in respect of 
the Treasury Solicitor’s in- 
vestigations at Shanghai. 
Total expenditure incurred by 
the Police Department. 
Total amount paid to Senior 
Counsel for the Crown in 
(1) The criminal trial. ; 
(2) The civil case, 
(d} Give a like answer in: respect 
of the Junior Counsel.

ASTUTE PAWNBROKER 

Mysterious thefts that occurred. 
within the last four or five days, 
have been brought to light. ‘In one- 
of the two cases heard in this,con- 
nection at the Kowloon Magistracy 
yesterday, the arrest was due in 
the main to a pawnbroker. He sus- 
pected a Chinese lad, who was en- 
deavouring to get rid of an electric 
fan, of theft, and accordingly tele- 
phoned to the Police to that effect. 

. The Police arrested the boy, and: 
the latter subsequently.made -his ap- 
pearance before Mr. T. 8. Whyte- 
Smith. He was charged with. the. 
theft of the electric fan, the pro- 
perty of the Club de Recreio, and 
also with the theft of two pairs of 
tennis shoes and a clock, which 
were removed from the Craigen-. 
gower Cricket Club, Happy Valley. 
Alternative charges of receiving, the 
above property of the two Clubs. 
were also lodged. 

Accused admitted the thefts. 

Det.-Sergt. Fitches said that on 
June 4, accused broke into the 
C.C.C, by climbing through the 
skylight of a window, whence he: 
forced a lock, and thus gained en- 
trance into a room where the clock: 
and the two pairs of tennis shoes. 
were kept. It was, in the: early 
hours of the morning that the theft 
occurred, ‘ % 

The very next day, the. accused! 
visited the Club de Reerelo, He- 
followed the same precedure in: 
effecting an entrance and stole an 
electric fan, valued at $50. A visit: 
by the Police to the Club later, in, 
the morning revealed finger printa: 
of accused ‘on a window ledge.. 

Accused. went: to a pawnshop in 
Yaumati where he endeavoured to. 
pawn the fan. But the pawnbroker;

THE ANNUAL MEBTING 


General Chen Ming-shw’s 
Opinions 
REVIEW OF THE POSITION © 
Carton, June 7. 
At the joint memorial service 
held in honour of Dr. Sun Yat- 
sen, on May 25 at Canton, Gen- 
eral Chen Ming-shu, the Chalr- 
man of the Provincial Government 
of Kwangtung, said that the re- 
cent danger through which 
Kwangtung had passed, as the re- 
suit of the rebellious actions of 
the Kwangsi generals, surpassed 
all past calamities. * 


Thanks to the able generalship 
‘of General Chan Chai-tong and to 
the exceptional energy shown by 
their co-workers and their com- 
rades-at-arms, the enemy was 
shatttered to pieces within a brief 
period and the Central Govern- 
ment was relieved of further an- 
xiety over the state of affairs in 
this part of the country. Due 
honours should be given to Gen- 
eral Chan Chai-tong for the part 
he has so ably fulfilled. The 
Chairman regretted shat owing to |!

foe. 


A Penalty Provided 

2 Under the present Ordinance 
a practice has grown up of return- 
ing an unused permit to the Har- 
dour Office with an endorsement to 
the effect that the permit has not 
been uacd. This is for the purpose 
of claiming a refund of the fee. 
There ts, however, no penalty under 
the Ordinance for a false or incor- 
rect endorsement of this kind it 
wae therefore proposed to jnclude in 
the amending Ordinance the provi- 
sion of a penalty for this purpore. 

3, It was subsequently found 
that other amendments of the Or- 
@inance were also desirable, on the 
following points :-— 

(a) Section 4 of Ordinance No. 1 of

which applications for Sunday per-

inatter: which would more properly

Schedule to the bill, because it 18

left ta deparmental arrangement, 
Section 6 of Ordinanes No. 1 of

once, 

Section 7 of Ordinance No. 1 of

made under the Ordinance. 

The sume sertion 7 makes “the

Section 8 of Ordinance No. 1 of

Ordinanee. The question of uiders 
and abettors is dealt with general-

4. In view of ‘these further

His Worship 
had been convicted on the first 
charge—that of conspiracy to ob- 
tain money by false pretences. 
He regarded Gifford as the worse 
of the three accused, as he was an 
educated man, Recalling the axiom 
about “honour among thieves.” 
His Worship remarked that to 
all appearances Gifford lacked 
that honour. In evidence, it had 
been stated that Gifford, when he 
saw the other accused were ar- 
rested, went up to them and 
pointed them out as the offend- 
ere, 

With regard to the second ac- 
cused, His Worship said that he] 
appeared to have been the person 
who had taken an exceedingly ac- 
tive part in the fraud, and ac- 
cordingly sentenced him to two 
months’ imprisonment, 

A Form of Blackmail 

Third accused, His Worship 
said, seemed to have put in some 
honest work, and if it were not 
for the fact that he had attempt- 
ed to extract from complainant 
some money, he would not have 
had a case to answer, and he (His 
Worship) would not have held 
that he had conspired. As it was 
however, he should have known 
thal the two other accused were 
wot representatives of the China 
Light & Power Co. His know- 
ledge of electricity, should have 
shown him that complainant had 
not stolen any electricity as was 
alleged, Therefore, it was yery 
evident that he did have a part 
in the fraud, which was a form 
of blackmail. His Worship said 
that he regarded blackmai) as a@ 
very horrible thing. Third accus- 
ed was sentenced to a monthy 
imprisonment,

his. iilness he was unable to do 
hia utmost; but he thanked all 
who had co-operated with him 
during the crisis. 

Having explained how it was 
throngh him that the Kwangsi 
generals Li Chung-yen, Wang 
Shao-hung and others had been in- 
troduced into the Kuomintang, in 
the hope that they might prove 
useful to the Party and the Gov- 
ernment, and how they had fail- 
ed to follow the path of righteous- 
nese, General © Chen continued 
as follows: : 


“Besides the Kwangsi clique, there 
is only one other person who dares 
to defy the Central Government: he 
is Fong Yu-hsiang. He has allied 
himself with the Soviets; made use 
of the communists; destroyed com- 
munications; embezzled famine relief 
funds; and utilised every means 
under his control against the orders 
of the Central Government. Can 
such actions be prompted by patrict- 
iam or by a love for the people? 
The Kwangsi clique has fallen; and 
it may be safely predicted that Feng 
Yu-hsiang will likewise fall. For he 
is not imbued with any principles, 
nor does he consider the welfare of 
the people. He has always been 
motivated by personal gain. 

Control of Military Power 

“At the close of the Northern Ex- 
pedition, and upon my assumption to 
office in the South, I made a declara- 
tion to the effect that the militarists 
having been defeated and the coun- 
try united, it was time to hand over 
all military powers to the Central 
Government, so that. no military men 
could interfere with politics. In 
comformity with that view, I resign- 
ed from the Central Political Coun- 
cil and the Canton Branch Political 
Committee.” s 

General Chen emphasised the 
importance of the civil authority 
as being above the military au- 
thority. The reverse situation 
could under no circumstances be 
tolerated. Such should be the 
case with the Central Government 
and also with the provincial author- 
ities, or else the period of poli- 
tical tutelage could never begin. 

On the occasion of his assump- 
tion of office as Chairman of the 
Provincial Government, General 
Chen declared certain principles 
which he was to follow, and 
which he would re-state, as 
follows: 

(1) The finances of — the province 
must be made public: a budget 
must be drawn up, and the or- 
ganisation of an incorruptible 
government must be speedily 
realised. 

The installation of persons in 
public services must be made 
public: ability should be the only 


criterion, and an examination 
system should be rigidly adhered 


to. 

Why Men Become Militarists 

According to the Chairman, 
there were three factors which 
induced men, with military power 
to ‘become militarists, namely: 

(1) Conceit and self-importance, 
(2) The desire to control territory, 
(3) The greed for. financial gain, 

Jt was General Chen’s belief 
that the militarists of the (1) and 
(2) categories would not appear, 
because General Chen Chai-tong, 
being a man of loyalty and’ prin- 
ciples, would never allow such a 
thing to happen: But-they had 
to guard against men of the third 
category, who would be filled with, 
the desire for accumulating 
riches. He did not mean to say 
that such inen were bound to ap-| 
pear, but Kwangtung was a rich 
province, ond past events had 
shown that whoever ‘had military 
power had ‘opportunities to enrich 
themselves at the expense of the 
public. 

While expressing a hope that 
General Chan Chal-tong would 
toke a firm stand towards hia 
subordinates and’ prevent the, 
finances; of | Kwangtung from, fall- 
ing into the control’ of the: mill- |

prison, supplies the fun motiva-

ous comedy which will be shown 
to-day and to-morrow at the 
Queen's Theatre

Mayer comedy feature, a broad 
yatire on “reform” prisons, the 
gridiron rivalry between jails pro- 
vides Many amusing situations. 
Dane, as the guard, tries to 
eliminale Arthur, a convict, from 
his scheme of romance by having 
him pardoned on the eve of the 
big football match with the rival

the game to win the girl he 
adores, Arthur srcks new felonies 
to commit. Complications then 
bring Dane and himself into the 
toils of the Inw and he is sent to 
prison.

SYD CHAPLIN 


“The Missing Link” at the 
Star 

To-day's big comedy at the Star 
Theatre, “The Missing Link,” pre- 
sents Syd Chaplin in a diverting 
role altogether different from the 
parts he has played in his other 
famous successes, “Charley's 
Aunt,” “Oh! What a Nurse,” and 
“The Man on the Box.” Begin- 
ning as a discouraged poet, des- 
perately afraid of animals, 
through a ludicrious series of 
mishaps, he ia carried off on the 
same liner as a famous big game 
hunter sailing for Africa to cap- 
ture the ferocious “Missing Link.” 
Throwgh further comical  inci- 
dents. Syd is compelled to pose 
ag the big game hunter. When 
they arrive in Africa the fun be- 
ging in earnest. What with the 
natives, the savage jungle deni- 
zens and the terrifying “Link” it- 
self, Syd has a very busy time of 
it, with plenty of thrills and 
laughs. Love causes another 
complication, and Syd must vin- 
dicats himself in the eyes of the 
girl, which he does in amazing 
manne “The Missing Link’ will 
be sereened for the last time to-day.

Francesca da Rimini upon which 
the photoplay is based, and also 
to interjeet into the screen ver- 
sion enough colour to make it ap- 
pear universal—in other words, 
he wanted romantic realism. 
Griffith has patterned his present 
photoplay on the — successful 
formula which made “The Birth 
of a Nation” the great picture it 
was. "Drums of Love’ has the 
same ingredients of spectacular 
splashes of action, with the addi- 
tion of emotionut passages that 
are fated to be named among the 
finest examples of acting on the 
screen,

Philbin, Lionel Barrymore, Don

J. FARRELL MACDONALD 


“Kiley, The Cop,” is the title of 
a new comedy whieh will be 
shown on Tuesday and Wednes- 
day at the Queen's Theatre, J, 
Farrell Macdonald, in the starring 
role, is cast as a jolly New York 
patrolman, He is gent to Paris 
to bring back a prisoner. In- 
stead, he finds a fun-loving beer- 
garden girl and meets with startl- 
ing adventures. Nancy Drexel 
and David Rollins, two popular 
young players also have import- 
ant paits to — play. Louise 
Fazenda, the famous comedienne, 
is ut her best in many hilarious 
scenes with Macdonald in the 
frothy backgrounds of Munich and 
Paris. The picture contains some 
excellent bits of photography 
taken. on the continent. 


Farrell Macdonald will be re- 
membered for his splendid acting 
ay “diggs? in “Bringing Up 
Father,” which was recently 
shown here. 

Sam Ku West and his Paradise 
Harmony Trio will appear at all 
performances in a new pro- 
gramme.

“FLN."—Victor McLaglen, the 
cinema actor, was born in 
London in December 1883, 
and spent his boyhood in 
South Africa where his father 
was Bishop of Claremont. 
He and his five, brothers were 
all over 6 ft. 2 in. in height. 
One of them was killed in the 
war.

succeeded, thanks to his

CHARM 


“Charm is an influance more 
than an attribute. It may be at- 
tained by cultivating the friend- 
ship of those whom we adore for 
that simple reasun, because they 
are charming. I do not mean 
that one may take it on as they 
might a bit of clothing, byt en- 
vironment has a great deal to do 
with it. , 

“T have always adored Norma 
Shearer. She has a certain mag- 
netism which captivates immedi- 
ately upon beholding her, not 
only upon the serzen, by Which 
medium she has gained countless 
admirers, but even on the street, 
in every day life. She has either 
leained or unconsciously acquired 
the art of charm, 

“I am a little skeptical 
whether beauty and charm run 
hand in hand, although it does 
seem that where you find one you 
often find the other. I am not 
assuming this as a criterion, how- 
evar for the personality of the in- 
dividual has everything to do with 
his or her uttractiveness, 

“Even clothes come secondary, 
for I know many very charming 
people who lack the niceties of 
life, but still retain a personality 
which endows them with charm. 
Strange ag it may seem, they 
could be wearing a gingham 
apron and upon leaving after a 
lengthy visit | could not truthful- 
ly say what they were wearing. 

“Ek do think that mannerisms 
have a lot to do with tha impres- 
sions inadvertently formulated of 
one. If there is one characteris- 
tie about a person which is un- 
pleasant or distasteful it is quite 
certain they will hold no appeal 
or charm for you, 

“Natuially evaryone admires 
and is seeking the better things 
in life and by keeping this ever 
before you your vory associations 
will automatically add to your. 
charm. If you are the least bit 
idealistic you may even impress 
people with your attitude and per- 


as to 


sonality which may be developed

A few years later he was a 
silver miner in Cobalt, Cana- 
da, and became’ the boxing 
champion of the Eastern pro- 
vinces of the Dominion. In 
Vancouver he had a six-roand 
contest with the black pugil- 
ist, Jack Johnson, which end- 
ed in a draw. Later he 
fought at the National Sport- 
ing Club in London. With 
his brother he took part in 
the gold rush at Kalgoorlie, 
Australia. 

On the outbreak of the 
world-war all the brothers en- 
listed in England. Victor 
served in Mesopotamia with 
the Royal Irish Fusiliers, rose 
to be captain and was made 
Provost Marshal of Bagdad. 
During his war service --he 
won several, army  heavy- 
weight championships, :

in “The Divine Woman,” which will 
be screened on Wednesday and

The story is somewhat unusual and 
concerns the adventures of a thea- 
{ricul star who, sickened by the 
homage paid her by wealth and 
royalty, returns to her first love, a 
private soldier and deserter from 
the French army. The picture is 
tull of tense and thrilling incidents 
and Greta Garbo is aaid to be more 
‘alluring than even in her difficult 
part; her vibrant and magnetic per- 
sonality standing out brighter than 
ever. Adapted from the novel 
“Starlight” by Gladys Younger, the 
story has been given e lavish pro- 
duction by its eminent director 
Victor Seastrom, and is told in ex- 
cellent manner by the large sup- 
porting east of players.

OUR WEEKLY CHAT 


On Cars, Trucks, Cycles And Accessories 


NOTES FROM THE FACTORIES 


[By “ Super-Six.’] 


Collecting London's Refuse 
It is possible to gather some 
idea of the magnitude of the pro- 
blem of the collection of house 
and trade refuse in London from 
figures quoted recently in “Motor 
Transport.” These show that 
about 1,202,000 tons of refuse in- 
volve annually 64,000,000 calls at 
770,000 premises. 
2 8 * 

Better Ventilation Needed 
A badly ventilated bus or coach 
may easily result in as much lost 
custom as inferior rolling stock, 
uncomfortable seating, or a 
schedule that is never maintain- 
Ven- 


careful consideration and inves- 
tigation when the body is design- 
d, and not after it has been com- 
pleted. It is up to the operator 
to insist that the designer gives 
the matter more attention than 
he does at present. 
. * @ 

The Anti-Dazzle Problem 

Considering the anti-dazzle pro- 
blem from all points of view, 
“Motor Transport” is of the 
opinion that all interests are best 
represented by some form of anti- 
dazzling lamp equipment that 
does not depend upon any con- 
trolling action by the driver, who 
may not feel inclined to study the 
convenience of other read users. 

so. * 

More Petrol From Coal 

A new low-temperature  car- 
bonisation works will shortly be 
opened in the North of England, 
says “Motor Transport.” They 
will be capable of carbonising 
1,500 tons of raw coal per week, 
and the weekly output will in- 
clude nearly 3,000 gallons: of

The Future of the Bus and Coach, | 


“We do not bollave that any 
one form of traffic can be adopt- 
ed to the exclusion of every other 
form, hut we do believe that the 
bus and coach have an immense 
future compared with which the 
extent to which they are at 
present used is comparatively 
trifling.’—‘‘Bus and Coach.” 

* * * 
Progress 

A writer in “Bus and Coach" 
points out that the first long-dis- 
tance coach service was started 
between London and Bristol be- 
fore chassis were built especially 
for passengers by the majority of 
manufacturers, while pneumatic 
tyres were by no means the rule. 
Now, four years later, Great Bri- 
tain is covered with a network of 
long-distance routes worked by 
coaches in which the passengers 
travel almost aa comfortably as 
in a first-class railway dining car 
at a fare that is exceptionally low 
in comparison. 

* ° * 


Cheapest Car in the World 

“The Rolls-Royce Bulletin” con- 
tains a letter from a Madrid 
owner:—“] have spent a few days 
witnessing the last touches being 
given to my chassis and have been 
truly impressed with the skill of 
the mechanics, electricians and all 
those who have taken part in the 
repair. At my request they dis- 
mantied various parts with the 
object of showing me the mechan- 
ism, and if hitherto I have looked 
upon the Rolls-Royce as the best 
ear in the world, after seeing the 
eare with which its various, parts 
are fitted Ido not wonder at the 
fame achieved, and consider that 


it is the cheapest-car in the world.”

The Influence of the Operator 
“The modern bus or coach 
chassis is, from the standpoint of 
design, becoming more and more 
the product of the operators.”— 
“Bus and Coach.” 
et e * * 
Diesel-Engined Buses for Britain? 
It would be @remature, in the 
opinion of ‘Bus and Coach”, to 
state that the Diesel engine has 
fully demonstrated its suitability 
as a power unit for passenger- 
carrying vehicles. Nevertheless 
most of the difficulties associated 
with its application to road trans- 
port have been overcome, and the 
writer of a letter in a recent 


issue of the journal expresses the 


opinion that Diesel-engined buses 
will be running in service on Bri- 
th roads within six months. 
“ f « 

“Baby” Challengers 

Admirers of the very small 
British motor-car will be intrigu- 
ed to learn that three “Baby 
Austins” have entered for the 
great road race near Belfast on 
August 17 for the “Daily Mail’ 
£1,500 prizes and the R.A.C. Tour- 
ist Trophy. According to the 
official handicapping these cars 
will receive five laps start. The 
circuit is 1834 miles long and has 
to be covered 30 times. The 
“baby” cars will have to compete 
with some of the world’s most 
powerful machines. Super-charged 
T-h.p. Austins have however, 
exceeded 80 mup-h. on the track, 
and with their handicap they will 
be serious rivals to the more 
powerful cars in this wonderful 
race, Their big advantage will 
be in fast cornering. The course 
bristles with sharp turns that 
slow down the heavier racing 
cars, = - 

ao } 8 & 


More Beautiful Cars 

The present trend in car design 
is towards the beautiful. One of 
the essentials of a modern-day car 
is good lines. In the olden days 
people were thankful to get a re- 
liable engine; lines and looks were 
ef secondary importance. But 
nowadays cars are becoming a joy 
to the eye; bodies are being slung 
lower, waist lines are being height- 
ened und wings given a more 
graceful outline. The vogue of the 
fabric saloon has introduced many 
smart bodies, and a number of 
models nowadays incorporate a box 
or trunk case on the back, which 
harmonises very well with the 
general lines if proper care is, 
taken. Such a car as the Arrol- 
Aster Six or “ Straight-Eight,” for 
instance, is typical of to-day’s, or 
we might even say to-morrow’s, 
fashionabie designs. Low hung, 
with a sloping screen, eliminating 
dazzle from thé rear, a_ high 
slender Tadiator, bow!-shaped 
lamps and wings which give the 
suggestion of speed and grace 
without sacrificing their shielding 
properties. This is undoubtedly 
the design of the near future, and 


Arrol-Aster are to be commended 


for foreseeing it. It gives the car 
that individuality which many 
manufacturers try to create, but 
which few succeed in capturing. 


Britain’s Municipal Vehicles 

In its recent municipal trans- 
port number “Motor Transport” 
drew attention to the very high 
stage of development reached in 
Britain in respect of the applica- 
tion of motor vehicles to the re- 
quirements of local authorities. 
In no other country have manu- 
facturera and users co-operated 80 
extensively and successfully to 
produce highly specialised vehicles, 
the result being that the means 
are available for carrying out all 
forms of municipal transport in 
an efficient and economical man- 
her. 

- 8 © 

Motor Cycle Speeds 

The results of the Colmore Cup 
Trial, the most important one-day 
trial of the motor-cyclist’s year, 
were illuminating. They showed, 
for instance, that ice and snow— 
even if the latter be of trifling 
depth only—can make tracks more 
difficult to negotiate than can 
mere mud and slush; for those 
who won first-class awards were 
few and far between. The times 
in the acceleration test, in spite 
of the film of ice which covered 
the measured stretch, compared 
very favourably with those of pre- 
vious years. This indicates that 
improvements in the gripping 
qualities of tyres have been made 
and probably, also, that manufac- 
turers generally have arrived at 
a better weight distribution on 
their latest models; without these 
improvements wheel-spin must 
necessarily have made the times 
very much slower than in the 
past. An outstanding perform- 
ance was that of G. Wright, on 
a 349 cc. Humber, who tied for 
second place, regardless of engine 
size, in 9.2,'5 secs., beating even 
such riders as the redoubtable 
Jimmy Simpson. Wright, indeed, 


“made fastest time in the 350 cc. 


class by 2/5 secs—a small 
enough time, no doubt, but in- 
dication of “supei-pep” in his en- 
gine, 

° * * 
Pope's Novel Car 


The Pope's new automobile 
from the Fiat Works at Turin, 
which was sent recently, is a 
spacious touring car, intended to 
be used for ordinary travel out- 
side State occasions by his Holi- 
ness. The car is Juxuriously fit- 
ted with four seats at the back, 
and two in front for the chauffedr 
and attendant. The Pope's seat 
at the back is in the form of a 
fauteuil, and the trimmings inside 
are of amaranth and crimson- 
coloured silk specially manufac- 
tured in. Venice. The other three 
seats at the back are so arrang- 
ed that their occupants will not 
sit with their backs to the Pope. 
The*cushions are covered with 
red velvet, and on the floor is a 
rich fawn-coloured carpet. The 
six-cylinder motor develops 40 h.p. 
On the outside is the Papal crest, 
and inside is a medallion in gold 
of St. Christopher, the work of a 
Roman goldsmith.

RAIL MOTOR COACH 


Cheaper Than Steam 
4 Toxin 


The Fiat Company of Turin, 
world-famed as motor car manu- 
facturers, has recently introduced 
an intetresting rail motor coach 
equipped with a six cylinder verti- 
cal Diesel engine developing 180 
erate electricity and drives the 
coach through electric motors 
coupled to the axles. 

This self-contained unit is very 
much more economical than either 
aleam or electricity on lines having 
comparatively light traffic. There 
is no costly installation, as in the 
cnae of electric traction; the Diesel ; 
engine requires only one driver, and 
there is not a big dead weight of 
fuel and water. 

The coach is nearly 60 feet long 
over the buffers, it accommodates 
8 passengers. has a Juggage com- 
partment, a postal compartment, 
and a lavatory. It can be driven 
from either end by one man, the 
controls belng similar to those of 
an ordinary tramear. The weight 
in full running order Is 44 tons, 
the maximum speed is 35 miles per 
hour, and the maximum gradient 
that can be climbed with a full load 
is 1 in 25. 

Tests carried out under working 
conditions showed that this new 
coach cost five times less to work 
than a steam train of similar capa- 
city, so that a frequent service can 
at a comparatively 


be maintained

CHRYSLER CARS 


Achievements During 
First Five Years. 


Mr. J. E. Fields, Vice-President 
in charge of Sales, Chrysler 
Corporation, writes :— 

The beginning of the year 
1929 also marks the end of 
Chrysler's first five years as a 
builder of motor cars. 

They have been significant 
years in the automobile industry. 
They have witnessed a great 
transition in the public mind as to 
what a motor car should be and 
should do—the complete transi- 
tion, one might say, a8 to what 
constitutes a proper motor car. 

These five years have wrought 
a complete realignment of motor 
car manufacturers. Depending 
upon how well they fulfilled the 
public’s wants have the various 
manufacturers come forward into 
prominence, held their position, 
fallen behind or passed into obli- 
vion. For, after all, it is the pub- 
lic's estimation of your product 
that determines the extent of 
your success, or makes your pro- 
duct a failure. This, of course, 
is true in every other business or 
industry 

In its first five years the public 
has bestowed on Chrysler a mea- 
sure of approval that has seldom, 
if ever. been equalled in the his- 
tory of American business enter- 
prise. In the period that public 
has bought more than 
Chirvsler-built ears. It has in- 
vested well in excess of $1.000,- 
000.000 in Chrysler 
To-day if is buying products built 
by Chrysler at the rate of several 
milion dollars a dav! The public 
has lifted Chrysler to a position 
of front rank among the world’s 


fareriost motor car producers.
